What brings you in to see me today?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 64 / not_in_workforce / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Hallucinations\nmanifestation: \"What brings me in is that I keep hearing clear voices in my apartment and sometimes in empty museum rooms, like women whispering from behind the walls telling me which photographs belong in the archive.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Reserved patient.\nA reserved patient may\n1) provide brief, vague, or evasive answers to questions about their symptoms and experiences,\n2) be reluctant to answer questions about personal information, emotions, or sensitive clinical experiences, and\n3) refrain from voluntarily providing information beyond what the doctor specifically asks.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

They tell me which photographs belong in the archive. It sounds like women whispering behind the walls, and I… I do find myself paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever directly tell you to do something, like an action you must take?\n\nPatient: They don’t say it like “you must,” exactly. It’s more… they whisper which photographs belong in the archive, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are ordering you to do something, even if it's not in a direct command?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it feels that way sometimes. Not like they shout orders, but the voices are clear, like women behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive. I end up feeling like I’m meant to follow that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices something you hear all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It’s not every minute… but it happens a lot in my apartment, and sometimes in empty museum rooms. Clear voices, like women whispering behind the walls.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices cause you any distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does. I get anxious when it’s clear like that, in my apartment especially. The whispering behind the walls… about the photographs and the archive. It’s hard to settle after.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are real, or do you think they might be a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: I… it feels real when it’s happening. Like they’re actually behind the walls, whispering about the photographs. Later I can wonder if it’s my mind, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that way.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like they’re actually behind the walls, whispering about the photographs. Later I can wonder if it’s my mind, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to keep your thoughts straight or express them in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My cousin will ask me something simple… like about a bill, or an appointment, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. And then I can see they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If my cousin asks something simple, like a bill or an appointment, I’ll start answering and then I’m on about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. By the time I stop, people look like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your way of thinking or expressing ideas is confusing to others most of the time?\n\nPatient: I guess… not all the time, but it happens enough.     "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22, F23, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’ve been hearing voices… mostly in my apartment. Sometimes in empty rooms at the museum too. They sound like women whispering behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever tell you to do something or influence your actions in any way?\n\nPatient: Yes… sort of. They tell me which photographs belong in the archive. It sounds like women whispering behind the walls, and I… I do find myself paying attention to it.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices ever directly tell you to do something, like an action you must take?\n\nPatient: They don’t say it like “you must,” exactly. It’s more… they whisper which photographs belong in the archive, and I feel like I’m supposed to listen.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like the voices are ordering you to do something, even if it's not in a direct command?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose it feels that way sometimes. Not like they shout orders, but the voices are clear, like women behind the walls, telling me which photographs belong in the archive. I end up feeling like I’m meant to follow that.\n\nDoctor: Are the voices something you hear all the time or do they come and go?\n\nPatient: They come and go. It’s not every minute… but it happens a lot in my apartment, and sometimes in empty museum rooms. Clear voices, like women whispering behind the walls.\n\nDoctor: Do the voices cause you any distress or anxiety?\n\nPatient: Yes… it does. I get anxious when it’s clear like that, in my apartment especially. The whispering behind the walls… about the photographs and the archive. It’s hard to settle after.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are real, or do you think they might be a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: I… it feels real when it’s happening. Like they’re actually behind the walls, whispering about the photographs. Later I can wonder if it’s my mind, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to keep your thoughts straight or express them in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My cousin will ask me something simple… like about a bill, or an appointment, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. And then I can see they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If my cousin asks something simple, like a bill or an appointment, I’ll start answering and then I’m on about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. By the time I stop, people look like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your way of thinking or expressing ideas is confusing to others most of the time?\n\nPatient: I guess… not all the time, but it happens enough. Like I’ll mean to answer something simple, a bill or an appointment, and then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. People look lost by the end.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disorganized or hard to follow for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I don’t know. It happens a lot, though. Like I said, my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I start with that, then I’m on the grant, and shipping routes, and my grandmother’s altar. By the end, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of thinking is more disorganized than other people's most of the time?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes. I don’t always notice it, but… people seem to get lost when I talk. Like with my cousin, I start on a bill or appointment and end up on the grant, shipping routes, my grandmother’s altar… and then nobody knows what I meant.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

It’s hard to settle after.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are real, or do you think they might be a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: I… it feels real when it’s happening. Like they’re actually behind the walls, whispering about the photographs. Later I can wonder if it’s my mind, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to keep your thoughts straight or express them in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My cousin will ask me something simple… like about a bill, or an appointment, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. And then I can see they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If my cousin asks something simple, like a bill or an appointment, I’ll start answering and then I’m on about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. By the time I stop, people look like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your way of thinking or expressing ideas is confusing to others most of the time?\n\nPatient: I guess… not all the time, but it happens enough. Like I’ll mean to answer something simple, a bill or an appointment, and then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. People look lost by the end.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disorganized or hard to follow for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I don’t know. It happens a lot, though. Like I said, my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I start with that, then I’m on the grant, and shipping routes, and my grandmother’s altar. By the end, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of thinking is more disorganized than other people's most of the time?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes. I don’t always notice it, but… people seem to get lost when I talk. Like with my cousin, I start on a bill or appointment and end up on the grant, shipping routes, my grandmother’s altar… and then nobody knows what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness, depression, or elevated mood?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes my mood goes very high. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something. But if someone questions what I’m doing or tells me to slow down, I can get… really angry. Like they’re trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Is your thinking usually disorganized like this, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when it’s quiet, I can still get tangled up. Like my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I start somewhere else—about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. It’s hard to settle after.\n\nDoctor: Do you believe the voices you hear are real, or do you think they might be a product of your own mind?\n\nPatient: I… it feels real when it’s happening. Like they’re actually behind the walls, whispering about the photographs. Later I can wonder if it’s my mind, but in the moment it doesn’t feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find it hard to keep your thoughts straight or express them in a way that makes sense to others?\n\nPatient: Sometimes, yes. My cousin will ask me something simple… like about a bill, or an appointment, and I’ll start answering, but then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. And then I can see they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself jumping from one idea to another when talking or thinking?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. If my cousin asks something simple, like a bill or an appointment, I’ll start answering and then I’m on about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. By the time I stop, people look like they don’t know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your way of thinking or expressing ideas is confusing to others most of the time?\n\nPatient: I guess… not all the time, but it happens enough. Like I’ll mean to answer something simple, a bill or an appointment, and then I’m talking about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. People look lost by the end.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your thoughts are often disorganized or hard to follow for most of the day?\n\nPatient: Most of the day… I don’t know. It happens a lot, though. Like I said, my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I start with that, then I’m on the grant, and shipping routes, and my grandmother’s altar. By the end, nobody seems to know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your way of thinking is more disorganized than other people's most of the time?\n\nPatient: Probably, yes. I don’t always notice it, but… people seem to get lost when I talk. Like with my cousin, I start on a bill or appointment and end up on the grant, shipping routes, my grandmother’s altar… and then nobody knows what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any significant changes in your mood, such as feelings of sadness, depression, or elevated mood?\n\nPatient: Yes… sometimes my mood goes very high. I feel almost radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something. But if someone questions what I’m doing or tells me to slow down, I can get… really angry. Like they’re trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Is your thinking usually disorganized like this, even when you're not hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when it’s quiet, I can still get tangled up. Like my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I start somewhere else—about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar. And by the end… people don’t seem to know what I was trying to say.\n\nDoctor: Is your thinking usually disorganized, even when you're not feeling anxious or hearing the voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… even when I’m not anxious or hearing anything. It still happens. My cousin can ask one simple thing, like about a bill or an appointment, and I’ll start talking about the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar… and then I can see nobody knows what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Is your thinking usually disorganized even when you're feeling calm and not hearing any voices?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think so. Even when I’m calm, if my cousin asks something simple, like about a bill or an appointment, I start going off about the grant, or shipping routes, or my grandmother’s altar. Then I stop and… they don’t seem to know what I meant.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time where you felt extremely energetic, irritable, or unusually happy for several days or longer?\n\nPatient: Yes… I have times like that. I feel… radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something. It can last days. But if someone questions me or tells me to slow down, I get very angry… like they’re trying to erase me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I feel… radiant, like I’ve been chosen for something. It can last days. But if someone questions me or tells me to slow down, I get very angry… like they’re trying to erase me.\n\nDoctor: Is your thinking usually disorganized like this all the time, or does it only happen sometimes?\n\nPatient: It… happens enough that it feels like it’s just how my mind goes. Like I said, my cousin asks about a bill or an appointment, and I’m suddenly explaining the grant, then shipping routes, then my grandmother’s altar.
